Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
50% load growth by 2035 on raised data centre load growth expectations 
We initiate coverage of the US Utility sector. With rising bond yields, the sector has derated in 
the past three months. Yet, fundamentals have never been stronger: We raise our US data 
centre electricity demand forecast and now expect electricity demand from data centres of 
c900TWh in 2030 (+80%) and 1,800TWh in 2035 (+94%). By 2035, we expect total US load 
growth of 2.2PWh, a c50% increase compared to 2025. We see an investment upcycle with a 
doubling in capacity additions by 2035 versus 2024/25 and tripling of electricity network 
investments by 2030 versus 2020. 
From theoretical potential to deals 
The past 12 months have been pivotal in translating theoretical data centre demand into binding 
agreements and we expect positive earnings revisions across the coverage group as utilities 
incorporate these deals into business plans. On our numbers, the coverage group average  
7-year TSR comes in at 12% on EPS growth of 8-9% and dividend/buyback yield of 3-4%. 
We initiate coverage of 27 new stocks 
Including NextEra, we cover 28 US utility stocks and rate 18 Buy. Our preferred stocks are 
Entergy, Pinnacle West, NextEra, NiSource, Sempra, and the four IPPs. We see five themes: 
◆ ‘AIDC (AI and data centre) high growth’: We initiate coverage on AEP, Xcel, NiSource, 
Sempra, Entergy, Evergy with Buy ratings and we rate NextEra Buy (TP lowered to 
USD103 from USD114 previously) on high (data centre driven) EPS growth rates.  
◆ In ‘AIDC compounders’, we rate Southern Co, PPL and Ameren Buy on resilient and/or 
above average AIDC growth. 
◆ ‘Generators’ could benefit from pricing and contracting upside and we initiate with a Buy 
rating on Constellation, NRG, Vistra and Talen. 
◆ ‘Reform(ed)?’ includes names with catalysts and recovery themes. We initiate with a Buy 
rating on Pinnacle West, FirstEnergy and PG&E. 
◆ In the theme ‘wires with overhang’ we initiate with a Buy rating on Centerpoint on high 
growth and visibility. 
We initiate coverage with Hold ratings on Alliant, CMS, DTE, Duke, WEC, Edison Int., PSEG, 
Consolidated Edison, Eversource and Exelon on below-average growth and/or risks.  
Why read this report? 
◆ On rising yields, the US utility sector derated recently, yet 
fundamentals improved. We double our data centre load forecast. 
◆ We expect positive earnings revisions as companies translate 
transformative data centre deals of the last year into business plans. 
◆ We initiate coverage of the US Utility sector and 27 new stocks. We 
rate 18 stocks Buy on sustainable growth in a data centre frenzy.

Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
4 
 
With rising bond yields, the sector has derated in the past three months. Yet, fundamentals 
have never been stronger: We expect 50% increase in demand by 2035, a doubling in capacity 
additions by 2035 (versus 2024/25) and tripling of network investments by 2030 versus 2020. 
S&P utilities P/E dropped to 15.98x on rising US treasury yields, but started to recover 
Source: LSEG Workspace 
  
We raise our DC electricity demand 
outlook by 80-90% 
We expect US electricity demand growth 
of 4-5% pa or a c50% increase in 10 years

Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
6 
For our US coverage group demand increases by c40% by 2030e and c90% by 2035e on 
average while high growth names guide for as much as a 60% increase by 2030e. We expect 
unprecedented levels of investments and double-digit ten-year RAB growth. The coverage group 
average seven-year TSR comes in at 12% on EPS growth of 8-9% and dividend/buyback yield of 
3-4%. We expect positive earnings revisions across the group. In our AIDC high growth and 
compounder themes we rate AEP, Xcel, NextEra, NiSource, Sempra, Entergy, Evergy, Southern 
Co, PPL and Ameren as Buy. Generators could benefit from pricing and contracting upside – we 
rate Constellation, NRG, Vistra and Talen as Buy. Across our ‘Reform(ed)?’ and ‘Wires with 
overhang’ themes we rate Pinnacle West, FirstEnergy, PG&E and Centerpoint as Buy.  
 
12% TSR on average with high end at 16% 
 
Source: Company disclosure, Bloomberg LLP, HSBC analysis and estimates. Note: (B) = Buy rated and (H) = Hold rated

 Approach & framework 
A feature of the utility sector is a strong interplay between Governance (policy & regulation), 
Social (economy, affordability, local infrastructure) and Sustainability (investing through cycles, 
transition and adaptation) drivers. 
Immense growth under close scrutiny with diverging local fates 
Source: HSBC analysis 
Who has sustainable growth 
in the data centre frenzy? 
◆ A feature of the utility sector is a strong interplay between 
Regulation, Social and Sustainability.  
◆ There is strong data centre and energy transition long-term growth 
potential. Yet, there is also macro risk, regulatory scrutiny and 
differing local fate 
◆ The sector P/E started falling in Q2 2026 on rising rates and 
affordability concerns 

 
9 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
Utilities provide monopolistic infrastructure locally which is supervised by policy and regulation; 
thus policy and regulation shape technology and investment choices. Public interest is high in 
local infrastructure and affordable cost for basic services which is reflected in local and federal 
policies. Utilities serve the economy and its industry while operating long-living assets, i.e. the 
utilities balance short- and long-term needs including energy transition and adaptation. 
The result for the US utility sector is three-fold: 1) There are large investment opportunities to 
power data centres and for the energy transition. 2) There is also close political, public and 
sustainability scrutiny. 3) The result is a new era of growth at fair returns on average for the 
sector; yet there is no one size fits all and fates diverge locally. 
Our 5-step company framework closely reflects these dynamics. 
◆ Serving the growth potential of data centres sustainably and socially fairly is the 
overarching change driver for US utilities. Operating in and expanding in markets with 
favourable regulation and policy (in general, energy transition and sector-specific terms) is a 
key consideration. 
◆ Resilient risk-adjusted returns and margins depend on company drivers such as cost 
management and digitalisation, but also on prices/contracting for competitive generation, 
regulation & policy for regulated utilities and resilience to extreme weather events such as 
wildfires or storms. 
◆ Effective use of cash. In a low beta (regulated and contracted payment streams) sector 
with high levels of debt, the balance sheet is always in focus. We assess sensible use of 
cash for capex, dividends/buybacks, M&A, or de-levering. We look for reasonably levered 
balance sheets and credit metrics and effective asset sales or sensible M&A with strong 
strategic alignment. 
◆ Execution quality is key especially in the current high growth environment: Does the 
organisation have a compelling strategy supported by its culture? Does it demonstrate 
excellence in executing its strategy and delivering its services. Is the organisation effective 
at understanding, interacting with, adapting to and shaping its situation and external 
environment?  
◆ Our valuation approach considers attractive valuation relative to peers, favourable macro 
environment and catalysts that drive stock performance. 
 
 
 
A new era of growth at fair 
returns on average for the 
sector; yet there is no one 
size fits all and fates diverge 
locally 

 
 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
10 
Sector overview 
Historically, the utilities sector has been seen as a bond proxy, demonstrating an inverse 
relationship with long-term sovereign bond yields. Higher sovereign yields make utility dividend 
yields comparatively less attractive to income-focused investors. 
This changed over the last five years in our view as fundamental growth, first from the energy 
transition and then from data centres, emerged. Especially in 2024/25, investors increasingly 
viewed US utilities as enablers of AI infrastructure growth, given the substantial electricity 
demand from data centres. From the beginning of 2025 to Q1 2026, the S&P Utilities Index rose 
by approximately 21%, while the US 10-year Treasury yield has decreased by around 6%. 
Yet, the recent rout in bond markets and US 10-year Treasury yields rising above 5% changed 
this. With rising bond yields, a cooling of AI enthusiasm and affordability concerns, the US utility 
sector has derated in the past three months. The S&P utilities P/E dropped to 15.98x at the low 
point versus 2020 and 2023 lows of 15.12x and 14.31x, respectively. Is the de-rating done or 
even reversing? Since the second week of October, the US utility sector P/E has shown signs of 
stabilising or even recovery. 
The S&P utilities P/E dropped to 15.98x 
versus 2020 and 2023 … 
… lows of 15.12x and 14.31x, respectively. 
 
Source: LSEG Workspace 
Source: LSEG Workspace

Impact of rates on sector 
The US utility sector is sensitive to US long term yields in two main ways. As a low beta and 
thus income sector, the relative attractiveness of the sectors falls with high bond yields. Further, 
as a regulated sector, regulated returns may seem less attractive in relative terms: the spread 
tightens between treasury yields and rather fixed or slow-moving sector ROEs. 
Financing cost may rise. For parent-level debt, rising rates may affect companies directly; 
however, utility companies typically have a very high share of long-term fixed rate debt and/or 
hedges, which should mitigate short-term exposure. Having said that, companies with weaker 
balance sheets, higher shares of parent debt, lower shares of fixed-rate debt or higher (re-) 
financing needs experience higher risk. 
Rising cost of debt in regulated entities is a pass-through. Yet, rising financing cost add 
incremental pressure towards rising electricity prices in an environment where regulators and 
politicians are already concerned about affordability. To offset the pressure, investment plans or 
other regulatory parameters might face more scrutiny. The pressure is higher at times of rising 
capital expenditures, as is the case now. 
Any future decline in interest rates would likely benefit the sector and our HSBC fixed income 
team expects US sovereign yields to come down slightly by the end of this year and then 
remain steady in 2027. (Please refer to HSBC Global Macro Strategist: Supply shock combo: 
What it means for markets for details.) 
 
Utilities and treasury long term yields 
Source: LSEG Workspace 
Risks of higher rates contrast with sector fundamentals that have strengthened, in our view.  
The past 12 months have been pivotal in translating theoretical data centre demand into binding 
agreements and firmer pipelines. We expect positive earnings revisions across the coverage group 
as utilities include data centre deals of the past year in their business plans and growth outlooks.

13 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
 
Unprecedented demand growth 
We raise our US data centre electricity demand forecast and now expect electricity demand 
from data centres of c900TWh in 2030 (+80%) and 1,800TWh in 2035 (+94%). By 2035, we 
expect total US load growth of 2.2PWh, a c50% increase compared to 2025. Circa two-thirds of 
this growth or 1.5PWh is driven by data centres. For our US coverage group demand increases 
by c40% by 2030e and c90% by 2035e on average while high growth names guide for as much 
as a 60% increase by 2030.  
A capacity and network investment upcycle  
Demand growth benefits every part of the utility value chain across generation and networks.  
In addition to rising volumes and investments, scarcity is improving the outlook for prices and 
margins – it’s a sellers’ market. We forecast renewables and storage additions to grow from c 
50GW of additions per annum in 2024/25 to c80GW by 2030. We see gas as a relative winner. 
Gas capacity additions had dwindled to low levels in recent years. We model gas capacity 
additions to rise to 20-30GW pa by 2030. In total over the next 25 years, we model c330GW  
(up from c200GW previously) of gas capacity additions. Nuclear benefits via higher prices, 
extensions, uprates and positive sentiment for small modular reactor (SMRs). We expect a 
delay of coal closures and estimate c50GW (from previously 100GW) of coal closures over the 
next 10 years. 
On our numbers, electricity network investments almost triple (2.7x) in our coverage group by 
2030 vs 2020 and continue to rise thereafter. Gas network investment could rise 1.6x by 2030 
versus 2020 on resilience, replacement and growth investments. 
Rising prices and affordability pushbacks 
We believe pricing debates and affordability concerns include the following: 1) Independent 
power producers (IPPs) expect rising power prices in ERCOT. 2) IPPs are de-risking generation 
via bilateral contracting and nuclear tax credits. 3) PJM faces affordability and new capacity 
challenge. 4) Data centres have potential to lower retail prices. 5) Electrification has long-term 
potential to lower energy cost. 
 
 
 
 
Higher than expected growth 
◆ Higher than expected demand growth – we raise our forecast– leads 
to… 
◆ …unprecedented generation and network investment upcycles 
◆ Yet, rising prices and affordability concerns sour consumer 
sentiment and increase political scrutiny.

New growth cycle 
Demand 
In our note, A New Era of Electricity Load Growth (13 March 2026), we expected electricity 
demand from data centres to rise from c200TWh in 2025 to c900TWh in 2035 and thus more 
than quadruple in ten years. Yet, the bottom-up aggregate of demand growth expectations of the 
24 regulated electric utilities in our coverage as of 2Q26 is vastly ahead of these expectations. 
In aggregate and based on utility deals and pipelines, we forecast c170GW of new load by 2030 
and c400GW by 2035 (predominantly but not exclusively from data centres). Utilities highlight 
that numbers have exploded in the past twelve months. This compares to BNEF’s 2H25 
demand forecast of c120GW by 2035, which the forecaster updated in 1H26 to c120GW by 
2030 in its base case and c160GW in its AI chip scenario. For 2035, BNEF now expects just shy 
of 200GW of data centre power demand. 
In aggregate across the coverage group, we forecast c170GW of new load by 2030 and 
more than 400GW by 2035e (predominantly but not exclusively from data centres) 
Source: Company disclosure, HSBC estimates 
 
BNEF and other forecasters upgraded data centre expectation in past 12 months 
Source: BNEF estimates 1H26

The US splits broadly into a dozen power regions (as shown below) and Regional Transmission 
Organizations (RTOs) or Independent System Operators (ISOs) are the non-profit bodies that 
are responsible for managing the transmissions systems and (if applicable) wholesale power 
markets in these regions. From a power region perspective, many connection requests continue 
to fall into PJM which hosts some of the original data centre hubs like Northern Virginia, yet data 
centre growth is broadening out with rising interest in other regions.

Growth forecasts for 2026-28 across regions in the US rose from very little in 2022 to 4% on

We expect US growth ahead of Europe but behind global and Asian growth 
Source: Ember, HSBC estimates 
 
Generation benefits 
In an historical context, 4-5% or higher demand growth has only been the case in 1950-74. We 
see four distinct periods of growth and technology leadership in the US across the last 75 years. 
◆ In the early days of US electricity (1950-75) power demand grew at an annual rate of c8%; 
it was the era of coal. 
◆ This was followed by the nuclear era: In the mid-seventies to the nineties load grew at c3% 
pa while the US built out its fleet of c100GW nuclear reactors. 
◆ The 3rd era was marked by gas and flat demand: In 1998-23, demand has been broadly flat 
and new gas plants start to replace coal.

22 
In our view, demand growth benefits every part of the utility value chain across generation and 
networks. In addition to rising volumes and investments, scarcity is improving the outlook for 
prices and margins – it’s a sellers’ market. 
◆ Renewables and storage additions growing: Among the different technologies, solar PV, 
onshore wind, and storage are quick to build with one of the lowest costs among different 
technologies today. Furthermore, these technologies have a running start with robust 
growth in recent years and supply chains that are prepared. Wind, solar PV, and storage 
additions in the US have grown from less than 20GW 10 years ago to more than 50GW of 
additions per annum in 2024/25. We expect renewables and storage additions to rise to 
c80GW by 2030. 
After flat or slightly down in 2026, we expect renewables additions to rise to c80GW pa 
by 2030. Gas additions rise to 20-30GW pa. 
Source: Ember, HSBC estimates 
◆ Gas benefits: In addition to renewables, we see gas as a relative winner. Gas capacity 
additions had dwindled to low levels in recent years. Yet, demand growth expectations are 
leading to a revival. After a 3-4 year ramp up phase, we model gas capacity additions to 
rise to 20-30GW pa by 2030. In total over the next 25 years, we model c330GW (up from 
c200GW previously) of gas capacity additions. 
RMI’s December 2025 analysis of planned capacity in utility resource plans (IPRs), which 
cover 47% of electricity delivered to US customers, shows that planned 2035 gas capacity 
has risen by more than 50GW to 95GW of gas capacity additions in the next 10 years. 
Plans for coal closures remain largely unchanged, if not slightly accelerated. Wind and solar 
PV additions remain the largest driver of growth in these IRPs with c210GW of additions in 
the next 10 years. However, expectations for 2035 capacity came down by c27GW.

Source: RMI analysis 
◆ Nuclear benefits via higher prices, with lifetime extensions and uprates for existing assets 
and improved sentiment towards small modular reactor (SMRs). We expect a 4-5GW 
increase in US nuclear capacity on lifetime extensions, de-mothballing and uprates.  
◆ Higher prices and demand create incentives for slower closures of coal facilities while 
closed coal sites might gain in value if they can be repurposed for data centres. We 
estimate c50GW (from previously 100GW) of coal closures over the next 10 years. 
Across our US utility coverage group, we expect the following changes in generation capacity: 
c260GW of renewables and storage additions over the next ten years, c180GW of gas, c4GW 
of nuclear uprates/de-mothballing versus c40GW of coal capacity closures – slowed down 
versus previous plans. This equates to c 40GW of capacity additions per annum with a c 40:60 
split between renewables & storage and gas. 
Of the 20-30GW of annual renewables & storage additions, c50% fall to NextEra as we include 
capacity from NEER in this view. For the US in total, we forecast annual renewables and storage 
additions of c70-80GW, which splits into market shares of 15-20% each for NextEra, US coverage 
group ex Nextera and European utilities; 5-10% for rooftop solar and 35-40% for other developers.

Affordability concerns; prices 
A number of states in the US have fully deregulated retail and wholesale power markets 
including California, Texas, the Northeast cluster (Rhode Island, Massachusetts, Maine, New 
Hampshire, Connecticut, New York) and part of PJM (New Jersey, Pennsylvania, Maryland, 
Delaware, DC, Ohio, Illinois). 
The four IPPs in our coverage – Constellation, Vistra, NRG and Talen – operate mainly in these 
fully deregulated states or their five respective power regions of PJM, ERCOT, NYSO, ISO-NE 
and CAISO1. Within these regions, market attention currently falls on PJM and ERCOT as the 
largest and more volatile pricing regions. 
The nine US investor-owned utilities (IOU) in our coverage that are predominantly wires and 
pipes – the mirror image of the IPPs  – also mainly operate in PJM, ERCOT, CAISO, NYISO 
and ISO-NE. This includes the two Californian pure-plays PG&E and Edison international, 
Sempra (California and Texas) and Centerpoint in Texas/PJM. Then there are the New England 
and PJM pure or predominantly wire/pipe utilities Eversource, ConEd, PSEG (some merchant 
nuclear), Exelon, PPL (integrated in Kentucky plus Invitium – the GenCo joint venture) and First 
Energy (integrated in West Virginia). 
The remaining fifteen names include AEP, which operates integrated utilities as well as pure 
wire utilities and fourteen fully integrated utilities of which three names – CMS, DTE and Alliant 
– have no meaningful exposure to electricity transmission. 
Part of PJM, ERCOT, NYSO, ISO-NE and CAISO have fully deregulated power markets  
Source: FERC 
 
 
1 PJM (Pennsylvania-New Jersey-Maryland Interconnection), ERCOT (Electric reliability Council of Texas), 
CAISO (California Independent System Operator), NYISO (New York Independent System Operator), ISO-
NE (Independent System Operator New England) 
 

 
 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
26 
We see the following debates of which the first two pricing debates are mainly relevant to IPPs. 
The latter two affordability debates are relevant to all, but more for regulated names and most 
for the CAISO, PJM and Northeast wire names. 
1) IPPs expect rising power prices in ERCOT. 
2) IPPs are de-risking generation via bilateral contracting and nuclear tax credits. 
3) PJM’s affordability and new capacity challenge. IPPs benefit from high prices and contracting 
opportunity while their regulated peers face the affordability challenge. 
4) Data centres have potential to lower retail prices. 
5) Electrification has long-term potential to lower energy cost. 
PJM’s affordability challenge, spiking capacity prices. RBP as the solution? 
With a market-based approach, PJM uses capacity markets to incentives new connection. However, 
because the process to connect at eight years is considerably longer than the forward procurement 
horizon of the capacity market at 2-3 years, the mechanism does not work as intended. 
Connection times increased to more than eight years in 2024 
Source: PJM Serial Service Requests processed by RMI 
PJM’s capacity prices for the years 2025 and following (set 2-3 years in advance) increased to 
USD270 and then over USD300 per MW from a low level of cUS40 per MW on average in 
2022-24. Prices for 2025-27 are thus 2.5-3x the 2015-21 average price of cUSD110 per MW.

Source: PJM, Monitoring Analytics 
With PJM’s capacity market failing to incentivise new generation capacity recently, the RTO is 
now looking to procure c15GW of new generation capacity via an alternative mechanism called 
Reliability Backstop Procurement (RBP), which as two parts. 
◆ PJM runs a bilateral matchmaking process intended to pair large load directly with (new) 
generation and utilities note that introductions have been made; conversations have started. 
◆ Alternatively, PJM plans to hold a central Reliability Backstop Auction for new capacity of 
c7GW with a ceiling price of 555USD/MW and c 15-year contracts versus 1-year contracts 
(3 years ahead) at a ceiling price of 325USD/MW in the capacity market. 
There is additional policy making including fast track approval of selected new capacity, PJM’s 
demand side policy on curtailment of data centres, the hyperscalers’ ratepayer protection 
pledge and governors’ interventions such as Pennsylvania Governor Shapiro’s Executive Order 
“Protecting Pennsylvania Consumers from Data Centre Impact”, which – among other things  – 
includes stipulations on bring-your-own-(new)-generation, generation in same zone as demand 
or energy and water use reporting.

If RBP is the solution remains to be seen. Utilities are cautious in their expectations and lean 
towards seeing bilateral contracting as a potential way forward.  
Data centres could lower retail prices 
If hyperscalers assume the cost of the investments, either upfront via construction grants, via 
data centre tariffs (that also have volume requirements), and/or via “bring (and pay for) your 
own generation” (BYOG), then retail tariffs should be ring-fenced from effects. 
In practice in 2026, we see many examples of data centre deals where the data centre 
developer assumes more than their fair share and combined with their large demand that 
spreads fixed system cost across a wider base, US utilities are increasingly highlighting how 
data centres can lower the cost for retail customers. 
Utilities highlight that data centres can lower retail cost 
Source: Company presentations and transcripts 
The US power sector has experienced supply cost inflation, a strong driver of power price 
inflation: Cost of key equipment in power has risen by 2-3x since 2020, due to a combination of 
macroeconomic and supply chain factors including general inflation, higher capital cost, tariffs, 
lower subsidies as well as a scarcity premium and risk premium for higher uncertainty. 
◆ The cost of renewables for 2030 is expected to double and even triple compared to 2020.  
In 2019/20, solar PV and onshore wind PPA prices were as low as 30-40 USD/MWh.  
For 2028-30e, after subsidies end, including tariffs and general inflation, at higher interest 
rates and higher margins for developers (risk and scarcity), we expect PPA prices to rise to 
70-90 USD/MWh. 
◆ Prices of gas turbines have more than tripled from 0.8 USD/KW in 2021 to 2.8USD/KW in 
2030. In addition, fuel prices have risen (such as nuclear fuel) or could rise according to the 
EIA (Henry Hub natural gas prices). 
◆ The cost of key network components such as cables, transformers or switchgear has 
increased 200-300% since 2021.

Power price inflation has not been even across the US: Large parts of the country have 
seen rising power prices in line with or slightly ahead of general inflation, but California (Pacific), 
the Northeast (New England) and Mid Atlantic (PJM) have seen higher increases. In California 
this is linked to networks and wildfire (prevention) cost and in the Northeast/ this is due to high 
network upgrade cost. These regions have traditionally higher network cost, which rose to over 
32-38% of total electricity cost in 2023/24 versus c25% in the rest of the US.

In the long-run, electrification lowers energy cost 
A shift to renewables and direct use of electricity could bring efficiency gains and lower total 
energy cost. For example, if households shift consumption to electricity via electric vehicles and 
heat pumps, annual spending for energy could fall to USD3,000-5,000 by 2050 (from 
USD5,000-6,000 in 2024), a reduction of 24-43%. 
The average US households spent USD5,000-6,000 on energy in 2024 
Source: EPRI estimates 
 
With consumption shifting to electricity, this could fall to USD3,000-5,000 by 2050 as the 
direct use of electricity brings huge efficiency gains 
Source: EPRI estimates

Grid investments upcycle 
The US is facing an electricity transmission and distribution network investment upcycle to 
upgrade an ageing network, serve load growth, and connect new generation capacity. In 
addition, technology progress provides opportunities for digitalisation investments. EEI 
aggregate data suggests that investment in US electricity networks will at least double by 2030e 
compared to 2020. Aggregated investments across our coverage group suggest that 
investments could rise by more than that: Electricity network investments almost triple (2.7x) in 
our coverage group by 2030e vs 2020 and continue to rise thereafter. We also expect positive 
revisions in upcoming business plans. 
EEI aggregate suggests that electricity network investments by 2030 could double 
versus 2020  
Source: EEI forecast, HSBC 
Gas network investment could rise 1.6x by 2030e versus 2020 on resilience, replacement and 
growth investments. Selected US states with high zero carbon emissions ambitions debate the 
future of gas networks such as New York, California or Massachusetts. Yet, in most other 
states, gas is a part of the growth vector on abundant and cheap resources. US utilities in our 
coverage that operate gas LDCs (local distribution companies) highlight attractive RAB growth 
of mid to high single digits and rising investments.

Regulation, policy and catalysts 
We categorise utilities’ regulatory environment into five tiers based on regulatory construct, 
commission and state politics, special situations as well as risk of changes – positive or 
negative. The framework summarises our view of a utility’s ability to earn attractive ROEs:  
It includes views on the attractiveness of the regulatory framework and parameters such as 
formula rate making versus rate cases, level of ROEs and other formula parameters such as 
riders or test years to name but a few examples. It assesses a utility’s ability to earn close to 
these ROEs with minimal lag considering rate making culture, commission and state policy as 
well as special situations such as California’s wildfire funds and politics. We also consider risk of 
positive and negative changes to the rate making environment. 
Utility operating environment tiers based on regulatory construct, commission and state 
politics, special situations as well as risk of changes for selected states and FERC 
Tier 1 
FERC 
formula rates, forward test years, not retail politics, transmission incentive adders 
Alabama 
RSE formula and automatic adj. within band, no litigated rate case since the early 1980s 
Wisconsin 
settlement culture, forward test years, biennial cases settled quickly. Stable commission 
Florida 
Clause recovery, storm securitisation, multi-year settlements with strong ROEs 
Georgia 
elected but pro-business PSC that has approved asks such as Vogtle cost 
Tier 2 
Iowa 
Advanced rate making with rate freezes and wind-friendly politics 
Michigan 
10-month cases, forward test years and negotiated 2040 transition; reliability audits 
Kentucky 
Fuel trackers, CCGT pre-approvals and coal politics aligned with utilities 
Missouri 
plant in service (PISA); future test years 
Texas/PUCT 
solid mechanism and business friendly; yet political intervention on resiliency 
Virginia 
rider-generous annual legislation 
North Carolina 
multi-year plans, performance-based ratemaking, some political influence 
Tier 3 
Minnesota 
Thorough and slow with contested rate cases; outcomes typically reasonable 
Colorado 
Good mechanism with some attention to wildfire, performance and green mandate 
Pennsylvania 
Fair with some trackers and reasonable ROE, settlement achievable 
Indiana 
Some trackers/pre-approvals; historically business-friendly, yet rate cases litigate hard 
Mississippi 
good formula rate plan with minimal lag, some politics 
Arkansas 
good formula rate plan with minimal lag, some politics 
Louisiana 
good formula rate plan with minimal lag, some politics 
South Carolina 
rate cases settle and average returns 
Kansas 
Settlement culture and DC-friendly legislation 
Oklahoma 
Historical test years and contested cases; trending positive 
Ohio 
rate case framework after HB15. Transactional but no forward test years 
Massachusetts 
good regulatory construct with below average pace and some conditions; future of gas question 
NH / RI / DE 
Small commissions, adequate outcome 
Arizona

Tier 4

We categorise utilities’

Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
34 
We believe our Tier 1 regions are well acknowledged top quartile operating environments 
including FERC (Federal Energy Regulatory Commission – the regulator for interstate energy, 
i.e., electricity transmission outside of ERCOT), Alabama, Wisconsin, Florida and Georgia with 
formula rates, settlement cultures, and long-term histories of constructive environments. 
In our Tier 2 to 4 rankings, we assume that the pro-business stance in Texas will prevail despite 
current political scrutiny of the DC and associated utility investment wave. We see Missouri and 
Arizona as states with positive recent developments. Broadly speaking, fully deregulated PJM 
states such as Illinois, Ohio, Pennsylvania and Maryland and New England states such as New 
York or Massachusetts rank lower than the quality of the baseline ratemaking framework might 
suggest on commission and political overlay partly in response to past scandals (such as Ohio 
and Illinois) but mainly these days on affordability concerns. 
Tier 5 includes California where inverse condemnation and wildfire politics dictate the debate, 
New Jersey where politics have broken to the negative on affordability concerns and 
Connecticut with a more adversarial commission stance. 
Company perspective 
For a company-by-company utility view, we aggregated the state-by-state regulatory tiering 
across business operations for an overall constructive versus contested rate making view and 
map it versus diversification (single versus multi state). We believe this summarises how the 
market looks at regulatory risk by company.  
Three categories of regulated debates: regulation is a) core of debate, b) monitored or c) 
not core focus due to quality or diversification 
Source: HSBC analysis 
We see three groups: Group one is a handful of utilities with more concentrated business 
models: operations or key growth segments fall in one or two states. A difficult regulatory 
environment or changes to it (positive and negative) are a key part of or dominate the investment 
debate. We believe this includes the Californian Utilities on wildfire risk, ConEd and PSEG on 
political overlay as well as Pinnacle West with expectations of a marked positive change.

The opposite is a group of utilities that are well diversified and/or operate in consistent or 
constructive regulatory environments. The market takes note of regulation and policy events, 
but it is typically not core to the investment debate. 
In the middle sit a dozen utilities, where regulatory developments form a part of the debate, but 
it doesn’t dominate as diversification softens potential adversarial shifts in one region or the 
business is more concentrated, yet the utility operates in a Tier 1-3 environment. 
Catalysts 
In the exhibit below, we outline selected catalysts. We see stock specific catalysts in the form 
of potential positive earnings revisions across the coverage at 3Q26 and FY26 results; NextEra-
Dominion tie-up or news on Eaton fire prudence and claims milestones for Edison Int.. US 
midterms on 3 November are a political catalyst with governorships and utility commission 
seats up for elections. Key regulatory events, in our view, include potential dates for passing of 
Californian wildfire legislation, PJM market updates (including capacity auctions, outcome of 
reliability auction and updates on bilateral contracting), updates in Texas on data centres and 
transmission build out and confirmation of changes in regulatory regime in Arizona.

In absolute numbers, AEP has the largest pipeline with expectations of 69GW of contracted 
load by 2030. AEP highlights that their estimates are conservative and if demand should drop 
out, this would likely be back-filled easily from and overall pool of request which is much larger. 
Nevertheless, as a large portion (45GW) of this pipeline falls into ERCOT and is based on LOAs 
(Lettes of Agreements) as defined by ERCOT’s Batch Zero process rather than more binding 
Electricity Service Agreements (ESAs), we take a haircut and model 70GW by 2035 instead of 
2030. Despite a pro-business stance, the wave of DC and related electricity construction activity 
has raised political scrutiny and landowner pushback in Texas. 
Relative to its size, NiSource has one of largest DC growth pipelines and we believe their 
GenCo (Generation Company that develops tailor-made generation for DCs) construct enables 
fast and efficient execution. NiSource highlights that their communicated 9GW pipeline is 
calibrated to the load that the system could absorb; connection requests run much higher. 
NIPSCO, the integrated electricity business in Indiana drives growth, while GenCo helps with 
execution and financing and the gas LDCs contribute high single digit growth and cashflow.

We believe communication around deals has been conservative and that the over 10GW of

Southern operates in Tier 1 regulatory environments.

opportunities in the updated business plan.

growth in the integrated business in Kentucky with 1.3GW of ESAs and 12.4GW of LOAs and 
from the Generation Company Joint Venture Invitium where the company has secured viable 
sites and inventory that supports 8-14GW of capacity. The balance sheet is very strong.

Generators 
Phoenix from the ashes 
The fate of IPPs in our coverage has changed dramatically in recent years from depressed 
prices due to rising shares of renewables and cheap shale gas to rising prices (market and 
contracted) as data centre demand creates scarcity. The group has also gone through 
transformative acquisitions, for example Constellation acquiring Calpine and Vistra, NRG and 
Talen also expanding their portfolios. 
In terms of business mix, Talen focuses on PJM with a balanced technology mix on our 
numbers. While Constellation’s acquisition of Calpine has evened out the generation mix 
between regions and technologies, with higher nuclear margins – especially when contracted – 
we estimate two-thirds of earnings in PJM and from nuclear. For Vistra, we forecast a balanced 
mix of technologies and between PJM, ERCOT and Retail. NRG guides for a broadly 50:50 mix 
of retail and generation with the non-Vivint segments evenly split between East and Texas 
short-term but shifting in favour of Texas long-term with new business of contracted gas.

We rate Constellation, Vistra, NRG and Talen Buy on contracting and pricing optionality.  
We see the following key debates. 
◆ Tax credits and contracting above market prices for nuclear is changing the risk profile and 
earnings outlook. We assume that nuclear tax credits will be extend beyond 2032 and 
assume continued nuclear deals. We model 75% of contracted nuclear for Constellation by 
2032e. However, we see affordability pressure and regulation as risks which could slow 
down contracting. 
◆ On scarcity and growth of demand ahead of supply, ERCOT power prices could rise.  
On the flipside, delay to growth, curtailment/flexibility and continued storage/renewables 
additions could keep prices below expectations. 
◆ Use of cash for share buy-backs, organic or further inorganic growth? We see options for 
selective continued M&A (at a smaller scale to previous years), selective organic 
investments such as NRG’s contracted new gas or Constellation’s 4-5GW opportunity set 
while share buybacks continue. 
De-risking nuclear via contracting and tax credits 
In the past year IPPs have announce a number of long-term power purchase agreements for 
nuclear – existing, uprates, extension, de-mothballing and a combination thereof at what we 
believe are attractive terms of 15-20 years, possibly inflation adjusted, and c75+USD/MWh for 
existing assets and higher for extensions, bringing assets back online or a combination of 
existing and new. 
Between nuclear tax credits, which provide an inflation adjusted floor at c45USD/MWh and 
rising, the explosion of capacity payments in PJM, which provide an uplift of c14-15USD/MWh 
to market prices, rising PJM forward prices of 60+USD/MWh and now long-term PPAs, the 
profitability of US nuclear has dramatically increased and been derisked. 
For example, we model Constellation’s implied price for nuclear (across all categories) to rise 
from mid-forties in 2022-24 to mid-eighties in 2029/30, while being materially derisked with a 
falling share of market price exposure. 
Questions on contracting include: 1) Will nuclear tax credits be extended beyond 2032? We 
assume this to be the case with nuclear enjoying bipartisan support. 2) Will affordability 
concerns and PJM’s Reliability Backstop Procurement (RBP) lower appetite for nuclear PPAs? 
3) Can the concept of PPAs be extend to gas or multi-technology?

IPPs expect rising power prices in ERCOT 
ERCOT 2027 forward power prices continue to trade in a range of 40-60 USD/MWh while IPPs 
report ERCOT forward hedging levels for 2026-27 of c50-53USD/MWh versus hedging levels of 
46-47USD/MWh in 2024/25. While ERCOT uses scarcity pricing uplifts and nuclear tax credits 
apply, there is currently no structural capacity market that is accessible to existing generation 
similar to PJM. 
An influx of renewables in recent years has kept power prices low and would likely continue to 
do so in low or moderate demand growth scenarios. However, in high demand growth scenarios 
– and there is reason to believe in those with a wave of data centres expected to come online in 
the next five years – IPPs believe that ERCOT prices could rise for 2028 and following years.

Reform(ed)? – event 
Reform(ed)? is a group of regulated utilities where we see corporate action or events dominate 
or play a key role in the investment thesis.  
This group includes Pinnacle West, FirstEnergy and PG&E, which we rate Buy, and Edison 
International, which we rate Hold.  
Pinnacle West has strong underlying growth from data centres and otherwise. The company 
used to operate in an adversarial regulatory environment also going through a period of poor 
customer service ratings. This is changing, customers service ratings have improved and before 
year-end we expect regulatory updates that will remove regulatory lag and move Arizona from a 
Tier 4 if not 5 environment to potentially Tier 2 on fundamentals, yet we categorise it as Tier 3 
on changes that will still have to prove themselves and due to an elected commission. On high 
underlying growth and regulatory catch-up, we expect high EPS growth and positive earnings 
revisions, possibly ahead of consensus expectations. 
FirstEnergy has strong wires-led growth across its businesses with a 25GW data centre 
pipeline, while completing a decade-long repair. Following the 2018-20 exit of the merchant 
business and 2020 Ohio Governance crisis, new leadership has rebuilt the balance sheet 
through asset sales and refocused the business on regulated activities. S&P upgraded 
FirstEnergy’s rating to BBB+ (from BBB) in December 2025 and Moody’s put FirstEnergy on a 
positive outlook. We believe the company will achieve 14% FFO/net debt within two to three 
years. We expect positive earnings revisions on data centre and growth investments combined 
with potential for multiple expansion on improving balance sheet.

The two Californian utilities PG&E and Edison International trade on California Wildfire 
risk and politics.  
California has seen among the costliest wildfires in the past decade, some caused by electrical 
utility equipment. By share of ignition, power lines at c10% have no outsized share in starting 
fires, human sources account for over 80% of ignition. Yet on destruction, power lines account for 
a much higher share of 40-50% of California’s most destructive wildfires. The reason is two-fold: 
◆ Coincidence of ignition and strong wind: Fierce winds such as the Santa Ana winds turn an 
ignition event into a firestorm. 
◆ California has high-risk WUI (wildland-urban-interface) neighbourhoods, i.e., houses built 
into wooded canyons and brush-covered hillside. 
Sparks that might not grow into a large fire (in the absence of high winds) or only cause low 
insured damage (as the fire burns ranch or uninhabited land) elsewhere, resulted in 
catastrophic events with loss of life and high insured losses in California. 
Furthermore, California has the unique concept of inverse condemnation: Property taken or 
damaged for public use requires compensation. ‘Public use’ includes public infrastructure such 
as power lines and extends to investor-owned utilities (IOUs) on the grounds that they perform a 
public function. It is a strict liability concept, meaning compensation is required irrespective of 
negligence. It is a cost spreading rationale that assumes utilities can recover the payments 
through rates, i.e., risk and cost are socialised via electricity bills. 
Yet, this link broke when the CPUC (California Public Utilities Commission – the state regulator for 
IOUs) denied cost recovery in a 2017 decision on SDG&E’s 2007 fires of Witch, Guejito and Rice. 
In 2018, PG&E’s equipment was found to have caused the Camp Fire, up to that point the costliest 
fire, and investigators pointed to years of deferred maintenance and inadequate inspections of 
ageing infrastructure. Facing significant liabilities, the company filed for Chapter 11.  
In July 2019, following Camp Fire and PG&E’s bankruptcy, California passed Assembly Bill 
1054, a package designed to stabilise the state’s IOUs. The bill included the following:  
◆ A USD21bn Wildfire fund – funded half by IOU shareholder contributions and half by 
ratepayer charges – to cover cost of future fires. 
◆ It capped shareholder exposure at 20% of a utility’s T&D rate base. It also introduced the 
burden of proof. A utility that holds an annual safety certificate is presumed prudent. 
◆ The bill mandated annual wildfire mitigation plans, alongside pre-emptive power shutoffs 
and expedited undergrounding.

California has seen among 
the costliest wildfires in the 
past decade, some caused by 
electrical utility equipment.

Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
46 
In the years since, PG&E has embarked on an extensive overhaul of its practices and wildfire 
mitigation programme, with KPIs such as ignition rates dropping dramatically. Subsequent fires 
where PG&E’s equipment was involved, such as Kincade in 2019, Zogg in 2020 and Dixie in 
2021 were much smaller in size and there were no meaningful events after 2021. Operational 
improvements seemed effective with the wildfire fund working as a backstop. 
Then, the January 2025 Eaton Fire happened, where Edison International’s equipment is 
assumed to be involved. Damages are still unclear, yet with the damages exceeding the wildfire 
fund’s capacity on some estimations, California passed SB 254 which includes the following: 
◆ A funding mechanism for a USD18bn continuation account for new fires ignited after 
September 2025, funded again 50:50 between ratepayers and IOU shareholders. 
◆ With Eaton linked to Edison International, the allocation between IOUs shifted with  
PG&E’s contribution share dropping from 64.2% for the wildfire fund to 47.85% for the 
continuation account. 
The Newsom administration characterized the continuation account as interim, pending a 
longer-term review and redesign of the liability regime during 1H2026 that from the utilities’ 
perspective should at least include a different source of liquidity/funding mechanism (without 
shareholder contributions) and a general liability cap (fund or no fund). 
The market expected these reforms to be passed by August 2026 and when that didn’t happen, 
PG&E’s and Edisons’s stock prices dropped over 20%. As indicated previously by the company, 
PG&E cut its 2027 investments by USD2bn and is reviewing its longer-term spending plan given 
financing uncertainty. Yet, believing there is political acknowledgement of its necessity, utilities 
hope the reforms will still be passed in 2026 under Governor Newsom’s administration or in 
2027 under a new administration. 
We rate PG&E Buy on energy transition and data centre growth and believe the extensive 
operational overhaul and wildfire mitigation program in the past five years has lowered wildfire 
risk. We rate Edison International Hold as the size of Eaton Fire claims remain unclear.

Wires with regulation overhang 
In this group we rate Centerpoint Buy on high growth and visibility. We believe that the company 
is on a path to improve its operational performance and that regulation in Texas will remain 
constructive despite political scrutiny on data centres as well as transmission investments.  
We rate Exelon Hold. We see potential for 50%+ demand growth by 2035e, yet believe this is 
offset by less constructive regulatory environments. For PSEG we see upside potential in 
contracting nuclear, yet we believe regulatory overhang in New Jersey and limited data centre 
growth lead to below average EPS growth. ConEd and Eversource rank at the bottom of our 
coverage group on data centre growth with higher political and regulatory risks.

The exhibit below outlines our approximated net income split by each step of the utility value 
chain, i.e., electricity transmission and distribution (as reported directly and estimated as share 
of integrated earnings), an approximation of earnings from differing generation technologies as 
well as gas LDC earnings.  
As electricity networks, renewables and gas generation capture the bulk of new investments 
and nuclear benefits from rising prices, the earnings mix across the coverage should move to 
higher shares of gas generation, zero carbon generation and electricity networks. 
Companies with c80% or higher share of earnings from electric grids and zero carbon 
generation include Edison Int., Exelon, FirstEnergy, AEP, Constellation, Nextera, Evergy, 
Pinnacle West, PSEG, Entergy and Constellation. However, within that list we assume that 
AEP, FirstEnergy and Evergy will continue to operate coal assets beyond 2031. We think 
ConEd, PG&E and Xcel also screen well: No coal or in Xcel’s case a path to closing coal and 
operations in states with high zero carbon power emission ambitions.

From a regional perspective, US states with high carbon free generation commitments include 
Washington, Oregon, California, Colorado, New Mexico, Minnesota, Michigan, New York, 
Massachusetts, Rhode Island, Connecticut, Virginia, DC, Vermont and Maine. 
States with the highest share of zero carbon power generation include hydro, nuclear and wind 
rich states such as Washington, Vermont or South Dakota at c80% shares, followed by nuclear 
rich states such as New Hampshire, Illinois or South Carolina and hydro/wind-belt states such 
as Kansas, Idaho, Iowa, Oregon or Montana with (60%+ clean generation).  
On the flipside, the states with the highest share of coal generation include states in the coal-
belt Midwest and the Mountain West such as West Virginia, Kentucky, Wyoming, Missouri or 
North Dakota. Gas generation plays a dominant (or key role alongside nuclear) in Southern and 
PJM states.  
 
US states with high carbon free generation commitments include Washington, Oregon, California, Colorado, New 
Mexico, Minnesota, Michigan, New York, Massachusetts, Rhode Island, Connecticut, Vermont and Maine 
Source: EIA, HSBC analysis

50 
Utilities confirm that data centres have not given up on net zero ambitions, yet for the current 
phase of expansion, speed is the primary concern and thus gas is a key technology to serve 
large load growth. As we now assume a further slowdown of coal closures and even higher gas 
capacity additions to serve a 50% increase in US load by 2035, zero carbon power emission in 
the sector seem out of reach by 2050 (let alone 2040) in the absence of hydrogen or carbon 
capture assumptions. Without those technologies, our modelling currently suggests an increase 
in power sector carbon emissions in the next ten years. 
On delayed coal closures and new gas capacity additions, our modelling currently 
suggests an increase in U.S. power sector carbon emissions in the next ten years 
Source: Ember, HSBC estimates

Our valuation approach reflects our assessment of a company’s outlook including (but not 
exclusively relying on) the following: 
◆ Changes versus the company’s outlook in the past three years with regards to both EPS 
growth outlook (including topline growth, investments, changes in the operating 
environment, margins, returns, prices or cost management) and risks (including company-
specific risk such as execution, sector-specific risk such as regulation and macro risks such 
as interest rates).  
◆ We consider the relative standing of a company in to its peer group and … 
◆ …reflect market sentiment and catalysts (such as earnings revisions or regulatory 
decisions) that could drive change. 
The sector trades on 12% TRS on average based on average EPS growth of 8-9% and average 
dividend and buyback yields of 3-4%. Above average TSR names achieve 16%. 
◆ Companies in the top quartile achieve TSRs of 14-16% on our numbers, which includes 
IPPs on share-buybacks and high growth regulated names such as Entergy, Pinnacle West 
or NiSource. 
◆ Above average TSR utilities typically combine above average EPS growth of 8-9% or more 
with 3-4% dividend yields for 12-13% TSR. Edison International is the exception on wildfire 
risk with high dividend yield. 
◆ Stocks with below average TSR are in a tight range of 10-11% TRS on EPS growth that is 
1-2% below the average which is not fully offset by higher dividend yield. 
We target or derive higher implied 2027e PE multiples for stocks with higher forecasted growth 
and the following matrix that plots PE versus growth shows an expected upward slope to the 
right for regulated/contracted names. There are five exceptions that stand out, the two California 
wildfire names on special situation and the three IPPs more exposed to market risk that trade on 
EV/EBITDA and cashflow yield - NRG, Talen and Vistra. 
Within the IPP group, Constellation fits best into the PE framework of regulated/contracted 
names on a high share of contracted/floor price nuclear, Vistra with some nuclear to a lesser 
degree, NRG even less and Talen least. 
 
Valuation approach 
◆ The sector trades on 12% TRS on average … 
◆ ….based on EPS growth of 8-9% and …. 
◆ … average dividend and buyback yields of 3-4%.

Regulated utilities 
For (predominantly) regulated utilities we use a PE multiple as our primary valuation approach, 
given the regulatory construct of RoE, equity ratio and RAB with other cost components as pass 
through. Our target 2027e PE, which we apply to 2027e EPS, reflects our assessment of the 
previously mentioned dimensions of change in growth and risk outlook, standing relative to 
peers as well as sentiment and catalysts. We value regulated names with 2027e PE multiples 
that range from 15x to 24x (-1SD to +2SD versus their past three year mean), implying 2035e 
PEs of 9x to 11x. Edison Int. and PG&E are the exception with deep discounts on wildfire risks. 
Our valuation implies 2035e RAB multiples of 0.9x to 0.5x for the group.

Integrated 
We rate AEP, Entergy, Evergy, Xcel and NextEra Buy with above average EPS and RAB 
growth on data centre and energy transition investments. We rate Southern Buy on DC growth 
in top Tier regulatory settings, Ameren on DC growth upside in improved regulatory setting and 
Pinnacle West on high growth with positive regulatory catalyst. 
We rate Duke, CMS and DTE Hold on compounding yet slightly below average growth. We rate 
WEC and Alliant Hold as well-understood quality compounders.  
Wires & pipes 
We rate NiSource and Sempra Buy on above average EPS and RAB growth driven by data 
centres. We expect positive EPS revisions. We rate PPL Buy on DC growth with GenCo boost, 
FirstEnergy on growth with balance sheet repair and PG&E on growth with easing wildfire risk. 
We rate Centerpoint Buy on high growth and visibility. 
We rate Exelon, ConEd and Eversource Hold on regulatory risk and/or below average growth. 
We rate Edison International Hold on Eaton wildfire risk and PSEG on regulatory overhang and 
low data centre growth offsetting nuclear contracting and potential renewables linked network 
investment opportunities. 
Generators/IPPs 
We rate Constellation Buy on nuclear contracting upside; NRG on pricing/contracting optionality 
incl. retail and GenCo upside; Vistra on pricing/contracting optionality and Talen on pricing 
optionality with open positions.  
In the following we group companies in these three categories of Integrated, Wires & Pipes and 
Generators/IPPs.

Energy Transition ● Equities

Alliant (LNT, Hold, TP USD69.50) 
Alliant is an AIDC play that is part of our low-risk compounding theme and we rate the stock 
Hold on 2026-30e RAB CAGR of 11% (2030-35e RAB CAGR of 8%) and 2026-30e EPS CAGR 
of 8% (2030-35e EPS CAGR of 8%) below the peer average. We forecast dividend growth in 
line with EPS growth on a stable payout ratio of 62% and expect an adjusted TSR of 11% for 
FY26-32e. We expect positive earnings revisions with our and consensus EPS above the top 
end of company implied 2029 EPS guidance.  
Alliant is an integrated electric utility operating in Iowa and Wisconsin, and expects a c60% 
increase in demand by 2031 (versus 2025 levels of c5.5GW) from 3.4GW of contracted large 
load demand. Beyond the 3.4GW already contracted, the company has a large load pipeline of 
4GW as of 2Q26. Iowa is the growth driver, and the company expects 2025-29 RAB CAGR in 
Iowa of 14% versus 9% in Wisconsin. 
The current 2026-29 investment plan of USD13.4bn allocates cUSD4bn to natural gas 
generation (1.6GW new, 0.4GW upgrades plus 2 BCF LNG and gas lateral), cUSD5bn to 
energy storage & renewables (2.3GW of new capacity) and cUSD4bn to network investments, 
predominantly electricity. We forecast Alliant’s capex to rise to cUSD4bn per annum in 2026-30 
with continued high investment opportunities beyond.  
We consider Alliant’s execution risk to be low for the following reasons: The company has a 
long-standing track record of strong execution and we believe the company can well execute 
versus the 60% load growth; potential further growth that could start ramping in 2029/30 should 
coincide well with the company’s ability to bring on new generation. Alliant operates in high tier 
regulatory environments (Tier 1 Wisconsin, high Tier 2 Iowa). 
We believe the company has a resilient balance sheet with a prudent financing approach and is 
on track to maintain its target FFO/net debt ratio of 14-15%.  
In our view catalysts for the stock include potential for positive earnings revision with 3Q26 results 
as recent data centre deals and further growth opportunities are included in the business plan.  
We rate Alliant Hold with a target price of USD69.50, implying 1.7% upside. We expect strong 
execution, yet slightly below average growth and think that potential for positive EPS revisions 
will meet consensus expectations (which are already ahead of company guidance).

61 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
Ameren (AEE, Buy, TP USD119.10) 
Ameren is an AIDC play that is part of our low-risk compounding theme and we rate the stock 
Buy on 2026-30e RAB CAGR of 13% (2030-35e RAB CAGR of 9%) and 2026-30e EPS CAGR 
of 9% (2030-35e EPS CAGR of 9%) in line with the peer average. We forecast dividend growth 
in line with EPS growth on a stable payout ratio of 55% and expect an adjusted TSR of 11% for 
FY26-32e. We expect positive earnings revisions with our and consensus long-term EPS 
estimates just above the top end of company-implied 2030 EPS guidance.  
Ameren operates transmission, an integrated utility in Missouri and a network utility in Illinois. 
We model a c23% increase in load by 2031 and the company highlights 60% increase in sales 
in Missouri by 2029. Ameren has c3GW of contracted large load and beyond that a pipeline of 
4GW as of 2Q26. Missouri is the growth driver, and we model 2026-30 RAB CAGR in Missouri 
of 16% versus 6% in Illinois and 10% in transmission. 
The 2026-30 investment plan of USD31.8bn allocates cUSD5bn to transmission, cUSD1.9bn to 
Illinois Gas, cUSD3.6bn to Illinois Electric distribution and cUSD21bn to Ameren Missouri while 
the company also highlights 2026-35 investment opportunities of over USD71bn. Missouri’s 
recently published 2026 integrated resource plan (IRP) increased new gas capacity by c5GW, 
new renewables & storage capacity by c2GW and includes 0.5GW of fuel cells versus the 2025 
plan. We forecast Ameren’s capex to rise to cUSD8bn per annum by 2030.  
We consider Ameren’s execution risk to be low to medium for the following reasons: We believe 
the company can execute the Missouri growth plan and Ameren operates predominantly in Tier 1 
and 2 regulatory environments. (Tier 1 FERC - Transmission, Tier 2 Missouri and Tier 4 Illinois). 
We believe the company has a resilient balance sheet and FFO/net debt ratios of c14-15%. In 
our view catalysts for the stock include potential for positive earnings revision as higher load 
growth and Missouri’s recent IRP are included in the business plan.  
We rate Ameren Buy with a target price of USD119.10, implying 12.3% upside given adjusted 
TSR of 11% for FY26-32e and potential EPS revisions ahead of expectations.

65 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
CMS (CMS, Hold, TP USD69.0) 
CMS is an AIDC play that is part of our low-risk compounding theme and we rate the stock Hold 
on 2026-30e RAB CAGR of 11% (2030-35e RAB CAGR of 8%) and 2026-30e EPS CAGR of 8% 
(2030-35e EPS CAGR of 6%) below the peer average. We forecast a dividend CAGR of 6% with 
payout ratio falling from c60% to 55% over time and expect adjusted TSR of 10% for FY26-32e. 
We expect confirmation of growth targets at the top end of guidance with our and consensus 
long-term EPS estimates just below the top end of company-implied 2030 EPS guidance.  
CMS operates gas networks and an integrated electric utility in Michigan (no transmission). We 
model a 30% increase in load by 2030 assuming 3GW of new large load. Of CMS’s large load 
pipeline of 9GW as of 2Q26, c1-2GW is in final stages, 4-5GW in advanced stages and 1.5-
2GW qualified; this includes data centres, manufacturing and semi-conductor load. Electricity is 
the growth driver, and we model 2026-30 RAB CAGR for electricity of 13% versus 8% for gas. 
The 2026-30 investment plan of USD24bn allocates broadly one-third of investments each to 
electric generation, electric distribution & other and gas utility and highlights USD3bn of upside. 
The recently published 2026 integrated resource plan (IRP) to achieve Michigan’s Energy Law 
Targets by 2040 sees clean energy resource additions of c20GW (versus previously 12GW), 
new gas of c3GW (versus zero before) and annual sales growth of 3-3.5% (up from 2-3%).  
The plan also notes the investment upside from this new generation, which will flow into future 
business plans. We forecast CMS’s capex to rise to cUSD6bn per annum by 2030e.  
We consider CMS’s execution risk low to medium for the following reasons: We believe the 
company today has a good track record, executing the reliability roadmap, and can deliver 
against the IRP and growth plan. CMS operates in a constructive regulatory environment 
(Michigan = Tier 2) with state net zero power targets, yet there is concentration risk with one 
predominant market. 
We believe the company can achieve its FFO/net debt target of mid-teens and model c13-14% 
FFO/net debt. We note Moody’s negative credit outlook for Consumers Energy. In our view 
catalysts for the stock include the business plan update with FY 2026 results which should 
reflect higher capacity and investments as indicated by the IRP.  
We rate CMS Hold with a target price of USD69.00, implying 0.4% upside given adjusted TSR 
of 10% for FY26-32e (below peer average) and EPS trajectory in line with expectations.

69 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
DTE (DTE, Hold, TP USD135.00) 
DTE Energy is an AIDC play that is part of our low-risk compounding theme and we rate the 
stock Hold on 2026-30e RAB CAGR of 11% (2030-35e RAB CAGR of 6%) and 2026-30e EPS 
CAGR of 8% (2030-35e EPS CAGR of 6%) below the peer average. We forecast dividend 
growth of 8% to 2030e in line with EPS growth on a stable payout ratio of 60%. We forecast 
adjusted TSR of 11% for FY26-32e; our as well as consensus EPS estimates are at the top end 
of company implied 2030 EPS guidance.  
DTE energy operates gas networks and an integrated electric utility in Michigan (no 
transmission). We model a 32% increase in load by 2030 assuming 3.6GW of new large load. 
DTE has already finalised 2.4GW of large load (all approved) with another 5-6GW of pipeline of 
which 2GW are in advanced discussions. The company expects to sign more contracts before 
yearend. Electricity is the growth driver, and we model 2026-30 RAB CAGR for electricity of 
12% versus 8% for gas. 
The 2026-30 investment plan of USD36.5bn allocates cUSD30bn to electric, cUSD4.5bn to gas 
and the remaining USD2bn to other businesses. The integrated resource plan (IRP) from 
September 2026 is tailored to achieve Michigan’s Energy Law Targets and foresees c15GW of 
renewables, c4.5GW of batteries, 1.7GW of long-duration storage and c2GW of gas generation 
(while coal closes). We forecast DTE’s capex to rise to cUSD7.1bn per annum by 2030e from 
USD4.2bn in 2025 with the entire increase coming from electric business.  
We consider DTE’s execution risk to be low for the following reasons: We believe the company 
has a good track record of meeting or exceeding earnings guidance in 18 of the previous 19 
years and can deliver against the IRP and growth plan. DTE operates in a constructive 
regulatory environment (Michigan = Tier 2) with state net zero power targets, yet there is some 
concentration risk with one predominant market, albeit a good one. 
We believe the company can achieve its FFO/net debt target of mid-teens and model c13-15% 
FFO/net debt which is above the downgrade threshold of c13%. In our view catalyst for the 
stock include data centre opportunities in the pipeline that will provide upside to the current long 
term plan.  
We rate DTE Hold with a target price of USD135.00, implying 4.1% upside as an electric and 
gas compounder with slightly below average growth.

73 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
Duke (Duk, Hold, TP USD123.00) 
Duke Energy is an AIDC play that is part of our low-risk compounding theme and we rate the 
stock Hold on 2026-30e RAB CAGR of 10% (2030-35e RAB CAGR of 7%) and 2026-30e EPS 
CAGR of 7% (2030-35e EPS CAGR of 7%) below peer average. We forecast dividend growth of 
7% to 2030e in line with EPS growth on a stable payout ratio of 65%. We forecast adjusted TSR 
for FY26-32e of 11%; our as well as consensus EPS estimates are in line with the top end of 
the company implied 2030 EPS guidance.  
Duke Energy is predominantly an integrated electric utility but also operates gas LDCs (Local 
Distribution Companies) and operates in North and South Carolina, Florida, Indian, Ohio and 
Kentucky. We model a 29% increase in large load by 2030 assuming 11GW of new large load. 
Duke has c15.4GW of large load pipeline out of which c7.8GW of capacity is already signed 
with 5.2GW under construction. Electricity is the main growth driver, and we model 2026-30 
RAB CAGR for electricity of 10% versus 8% for gas. From a regional perspective, the Carolinas 
are the fastest growing region with a 2026-30e RAB CAGR of 12%. Yet, the company notes 
potential for USD5-10bn of additional capital to serve large loads in Indiana and Florida. 
The current 2026-30 investment plan of USD103bn allocates USD40bn to electric grids, 
cUSD51bn to electricity generation targeting c15GW of new capacity by 2031e, cUSD8bn in 
gas infrastructure and the remaining to environmental and other businesses. We forecast 
Duke’s capex to rise to cUSD22.7bn per annum by 2030e from USD14.8bn in 2025 with most of 
the increase coming from electric generation and grids.  
We consider Duke’s execution risk to be low to medium for the following reasons: We believe 
the company today has a good track record of meeting or exceeding earnings guidance and can 
deliver against its growth plan. The company operates in multiple geographies with Florida in 
Tier 1 and rest of the regions in Tier2/3 – consistent or constructive regulatory environments. 
We believe the company has a resilient balance sheet and can achieve its FFO/net debt target 
of c14.5% which is above the downgrade threshold of c12.5%.  
In our view catalysts for the stock include data centre opportunities that could provide upside.  
We rate Duke Energy Hold with a target price of USD123.00, implying 1.3% upside on EPS 
growth slightly behind high growth peers.

77 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
Entergy (ETR, Buy, TP USD123.50) 
Entergy is an AIDC play that is part of our high growth theme and we rate the stock Buy on 
2026-30e RAB CAGR of 16% (2030-35e RAB CAGR of 10%) and 2026-30e EPS CAGR of 13% 
(2030-35e EPS CAGR of 12%) in the top-quartile. We forecast dividend growth of 13% to 2030e 
in line with EPS growth on a stable payout ratio of 63%. We forecast adjusted TSR for FY26-
32e of 16%; our as well as consensus EPS estimates are in line with company implied 2030 
EPS guidance.  
Entergy is an integrated electric utility operating in Louisiana, Arkansas, Texas, Mississippi and 
New Orleans. Louisiana drives Entergy’s growth, and the company expects c60% demand 
growth by 2030 highlighting ample investment opportunities beyond. We model a c53% 
increase in large load by 2030.  
The 2026-30 investment plan of USD67bn allocates cUSD20bn to electric grids, USD7bn to 
renewables and storage, USD6bn to baseload generation, cUSD31bn to other new generation 
including CCGTs and USD2bn to other businesses. The company plans to add c30GW of new 
capacity during 2026-35. We note management’s execution confidence based on early 
management of supply chain with reservation of turbine slots, standardised CCGTs and strategic 
EPC partnerships. We forecast Entergy’s capex to rise to cUSD15.6bn per annum by 2030e from 
USD8.4bn in 2025 with the increase mostly coming from electric grids and baseload generation.  
Upfront payments from data centres support a resilient balance sheet and the company expects 
improving FFO/net debt credit ratios over the next five years. Our FFO/debt estimates sit 
comfortably above the credit rating downgrade threshold of 13%. In our view catalysts for the 
stock include further growth options and potential investment revisions on the back of it. 
We consider Entergy’s execution risk to be low for the following reasons: We believe the 
company is slightly ahead in the race for supply capacity and benefits from the scale of a 
standardised process as well as close collaboration with DC clients. The company operates in 
reasonably constructive regulatory environment which are ranked in Tier 2 and 3 in our analysis. 
We rate Entergy Buy with a target price of USD123.50, implying 17.5% upside on top quartile 
growth in resilient regulatory environments.

81 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
Evergy (EVRG, Buy, TP USD92.0) 
Evergy is an AIDC play as part of our high growth theme, and we rate the stock Buy on 2026-
30e RAB CAGR of 12% (2030-35e RAB CAGR of 8%) and 2026-30e EPS CAGR of 8% (2030-
35e EPSCAGR of 8%) slightly below the peer average. We forecast dividend growth of 4% to 
2030e behind EPS growth on a payout ratio 50-60 %. We forecast adjusted TSR for FY26-32e 
of 12%; our as well as consensus EPS estimates are at the upper end of company implied 2030 
EPS guidance. 
Evergy is an integrated utility operating in Kansas and Missouri and we forecast a c40% 
increase in demand by 2030 (versus 2025). The large load pipeline amounts to over 16GW with 
3GW active operations & signed ESAs, 2-2.5GW expansion opportunities, 1-2GW in advanced 
discussions and 10GW incremental pipeline. Missouri is the growth driver, and we model 2025-
30e RAB CAGR in Missouri of 23% versus 9% in Metro (Kansas City) and 8% in Kansas 
Central. The company notes that demand accelerated in past six months and that Kansas is a 
new hotspot. Existing partners want to expand sites, which could happen by 2029/30, while new 
customers could ramp by 2032+. 
The 2026-30 investment plan of USD21.6bn allocates cUSD9bn to new generation/renewables 
(over 3GW of gas and 1GW of solar & batteries), cUSD4bn to Transmission, cUSD5bn to 
Distribution and the rest to IT/Other/Legacy Generation. We forecast Evergy’s capex to rise to 
cUSD5.5bn per annum by 2030e on investments for growth and resilience. The company notes 
that the integrated resource plans filed in 2026 offer USD1bn upside to the business plan from 
February 2026.  
We consider Evergy’s execution risk to be low to medium for the following reasons: We think 
guidance misses of previous years (on weather and rising cost) are in the past and the 
company can execute against the growth plan. Evergy operates in above average regulatory 
environments (Missouri – growth driver – Tier 2, Kansas Tier 3). 
We believe the company has a resilient balance sheet with supportive large load contracts and 
we expect FFO/net debt of 14-15% within the company’s target range. 
In our view, catalysts for the stock include future business plan updates with potential increases 
in investments and positive earnings revisions.  
We rate Evergy Buy with a target price of USD92.00, implying 10.4% upside on attractive 
growth from data centres.

85 
Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
Nextera (NEE, Buy, TP USD103.00) 
Nextera is an AIDC play as part of our high growth theme, and we rate the stock Buy on 2026-
32e EPS CAGR of 10% above the peer average. We forecast dividend growth of 7% to 2032e 
slightly behind EPS growth on a declining payout ratio in the range of 56-61%. We forecast 
adjusted TSR for FY26-32e of 13%; our as well as consensus EPS estimates are at the upper 
end of company implied 2030 EPS guidance. 
In its business plan over the period 2025-32, Nextera targets capex of USD295-325bn with 
USD90-100bn in its FPL business and USD185-225bn in the NEER business. We expect US 
electricity demand to rise by 50% in the next 10 years, driven by data centres and other sources.  
We think NextEra is uniquely placed to benefit with its scale stand-alone and even more so after 
the potential tie-up with Dominion. We see NextEra as a technology leader, and the use of AI in its 
own and Dominion’s operations should maintain or even expand operational and cost leadership.  
We believe NextEra’s 8%+ stand-alone (or 9%+ post-deal) EPS growth target is conservative 
and well-funded. Nextera Energy Resources (NEER) can offer comprehensive transmission and 
all of the above generation solutions. We estimate NextEra should reach an EPS CAGR of c9% 
for 2026-32e (pre-deal, NEER 14%, FPL 7%) and see NEER on track for >14GW of gas and 
renewables additions per year in 2026-32. Florida Power & Light (FPL) continues its path of 
high-single-digit rate base growth.  
We see NextEra’s growth ambitions as well-funded, mainly through debt issuances, asset level 
financing, and its operating cashflow. Nextera has a strong balance sheet with the company 
ranking 1st in adjusted FFO/debt and debt/adj. EBITDA among the utility companies in the S&P 
500 (source: Nextera). 
We rate Nextera Buy with a target price of USD103.00, implying 29.1% upside as an efficient 
capital allocator in a fast growing US network and generation market.

Energy Transition ● Equities
October 2026
88 
Pinnacle West (PNW, Buy, TP USD119.5) 
Pinnacle West is an AIDC play and part of our Reform(ed)? theme; we rate the stock Buy on 
2026-30e RAB CAGR of 12% (2030-35e RAB CAGR of 10%) and 2026-30e EPS CAGR of 13% 
(2030-35e EPS CAGR of 8%) above the peer average. We forecast dividend growth of 11% to 
2030e broadly in line with EPS growth on a payout ratio of 65-75%. We forecast adjusted TSR 
for FY26-32e of 16%; our as well as consensus EPS estimates are in the top half of company 
implied 2028 EPS guidance.  
Pinnacle West is an integrated utility operating in Arizona and we forecast a c45% increase in 
demand by 2030 (versus 2025). The company notes potential for over 20GW of new load and 
has recorded growth rates of 5-10% in the last six quarters, ahead of increased guidance of  
5-7% annual growth through 2030. We model 2025-30e RAB CAGR for Transmission of 17% 
versus 11% at APS. 
The 2026-28 investment plan of USD10.4bn – USD2.65bn pa – allocates cUSD800m pa to 
Generation, cUSD700m pa to Transmission and cUSD750m pa to Distribution. We forecast 
Pinnacle West’s capex to rise to over cUSD4bn per annum by 2030e as an expected removal of 
regulatory lag lets the company invest for growth.  
We consider Pinnacle West’s execution risk to be medium to high for the following reasons: The 
company’s past includes under-earning on regulatory lag, adversarial regulatory relationships 
and periods of poor customer satisfaction. This has changed in more recent years as the 
company has worked hard to overhaul its processes. The final step is a pivotal ratemaking 
decision before YE 2026, which should dramatically improve the company’s ability to earn its 
allowed ROE. We rate Arizona a Tier 3 regulatory environment (historically well below average) 
on the expectations of material improvements. 
We believe the company has a resilient balance sheet and we forecast FFO/net debt of 14-15% 
in the next five years within the company’s targeted range of 14-16%. 
In our view, the company has important catalysts in 4Q26 with midterm elections that include 
two ACC seats as well as the ACC’s decision on the pivotal rate case. Following an affirmative 
regulatory decision, we expect the company to catch up on regulatory lag over the following two 
to three years and publish an updated business plan. We expect upward revision of investments 
and positive earnings revisions. 
We rate Pinnacle West Buy with a target price of USD119.50, implying 15.5% upside on high 
growth, improving regulation and EPS revision upside.
